# Reflection Layer (/materials-shading/shading-and-reflection/reflection-layer)

> Reflect the surrounding scene in a surface — a polished floor, a mirror, wet ground.



The **Reflection Layer** makes a surface reflect the rest of your scene. Use it for
polished floors, wet ground, glossy tabletops and mirrors.

Unlike the [Glass Layer](https://docs.spline.design/materials-shading/shading-and-reflection/glass-layer.md), which is about seeing
*through* a surface, this is about seeing the scene bounced *off* it.

<Callout>
  Reflections render on the **WebGPU** graphics pipeline. On a browser without WebGPU
  support, which falls back to WebGL, the layer will not appear — so set your scene's
  **Renderer** accordingly before publishing.
  See [Play Settings](https://docs.spline.design/exporting-your-scene/play-settings.md).
</Callout>

***

## Adding the layer [#adding-the-layer]

Select an object, then in the **Material*&#x2A; section of the inspector click &#x2A;*+** and
choose **Reflection**.

***

## Choosing a Surface mode [#choosing-a-surface-mode]

The **Surface** control is the first decision, because it changes what the
reflection can show:

| Mode       | Use it for                                | What it can show                                                  |
| ---------- | ----------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Shape**  | Any geometry — curved, irregular, sloped  | Whatever is visible on screen                                     |
| **Mirror** | Flat surfaces: floors, walls, still water | A true reflection, including things the camera can't see directly |

**Shape** works on anything and is the right default. It reflects what's currently on
screen, so an object's hidden underside, or something just off the edge of the frame,
won't appear in the reflection.

**Mirror** is a genuine reflection rendered from the mirrored viewpoint, so it *can*
show an object's underside in a floor, or scenery outside the frame. It's meant for
flat surfaces.

<Callout type="warn">
  **One Mirror surface per scene.** If several objects use Mirror, the largest one gets
  the true reflection and the rest fall back to Shape. Plan for a single hero
  reflective surface — a floor or a water plane — rather than several.
</Callout>

***

## Controls [#controls]

| Control        | What it does                                                                                                                     |
| -------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Blur**       | Softens the reflection. Raise it for brushed metal, polished concrete or a rough wet surface; keep it at zero for a clean mirror |
| **Thickness**  | How thick the reflecting surface reads as being                                                                                  |
| **Steps**      | How far the reflection searches through the scene. Higher catches more distant detail at more cost                               |
| **Steps Size** | How finely it searches. Smaller is more accurate, larger is cheaper and can skip thin objects                                    |
| **Tracing**    | The search method — **Linear** or **Hi-Z**. Hi-Z generally holds up better over long distances                                   |

**Blur**, **Thickness**, **Steps**, **Step Size** and **Tracing** apply to the
**Shape** mode. A Mirror surface renders a true reflection and doesn't need them.

***

## Tips [#tips]

* **Blur does the heavy lifting.** Perfectly sharp reflections read as artificial in
  most scenes; a little blur is usually more convincing and cheaper.
* **If a reflection has gaps or streaks**, raise **Steps** or reduce **Step Size** —
  that's the search missing geometry.
* **Reflections cost performance.** On a scene that's slowing down, reduce Steps
  first. See
  [How to optimize your scene](https://docs.spline.design/exporting-your-scene/how-to-optimize-your-scene.md).
* **Wet ground:** a dark base colour, moderate blur and a low roughness surface gets
  you most of the way there.


# Toon Layer (/materials-shading/shading-and-reflection/toon-layer)



The toon layer can be used in combination with the [Outline Layer](https://docs.spline.design/materials-shading/surface-detail/outline-layer.md) to create hand-drawn style effects.

<Embed src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/5tq9R2zQxYg" title="YouTube video player" />

The following parameters can be adjusted:

* **Position** - The position where the gradient is pointing, the default is set to camera but it can be changed to follow the lights or to be static.
* **Gradient** - Affects the shading of the object.
* **Offset** - Changes the gradient location from the object origin.
* **Noise** - Strength of the noise effect.
* **Noise Scale** - Controls the noise frequency scale.

<Image src="https://cdn.spline.design/_assets/docs/e179fc30-e5b1-4e84-addc-29e9ec9d76ab.png" alt="image" maxWidth="300" />


# Bump Map & Roughness Map (/materials-shading/surface-detail/bump-map-roughness-map)



Bump Map and Roughness Map create the illusion of depth and detail, making the material look more realistic without changing the object’s geometry. This way you can add details to your object without increasing polygons and affecting the performance of your scene.

## How to use a Bump Map [#how-to-use-a-bump-map]

A bump map is an image from which its luminance date (lighter/darker areas) are used to simulate concavity on the surface of your shapes. Spline will automatically capture the luminance from any image layer (color or black-and-white) and use it as the map.

1. Add an image layer with your bump map.
2. On the lighting layer, select either Phong or Physical.
3. Click on the lighting layer to assign a Bump Map to it.
4. Select the image you want to use as a Bump Map from the dropdown on the lightning layer's Bump map section.
5. Use the intensity parameter to control the appearance of the bump map.

<Video src="https://cdn.spline.design/_assets/docs/74b6d67d-3281-4e71-a4f5-7f01b4429d4d.mp4" />

## How to use a Roughness Map [#how-to-use-a-roughness-map]

A roughness map helps control how shiny or rough a surface appears in a 3D object. It uses the luminance (light/dark areas) of an image to guide how light interacts with the surface, making lighter areas look smooth and reflective and darker areas look rough and less reflective. Spline will automatically capture the luminance from any image layer (color or black-and-white) and use it as the map.

1. Add an image layer with your Roughness Map.
2. On the lighting layer, select Physical.
3. Click on the lighting layer to assign a Roughness Map to it
4. Select the image you want to use as a Roughness Map from the dropdown on the lightning layer's Rough Map section.
5. Use the Roughness, Metalness, and Reflectivity parameters to control the appearance of the Roughness Map.

<Video src="https://cdn.spline.design/_assets/docs/9d88a17c-a910-47bd-8803-4bfbfaa2bf15.mp4" />


# Cavity Layer (/materials-shading/surface-detail/cavity-layer)

> Bring out an object's shape by shading its ridges and recesses.



The **Cavity Layer** shades an object according to the curvature of its surface:
raised edges get one treatment, recessed areas another. It's what makes carved
detail, panel gaps and sculpted forms legible instead of flattening out under even
lighting.

Useful on sculpted meshes, imported CAD parts and anything with fine surface detail
that isn't reading clearly.

***

## Adding the layer [#adding-the-layer]

Select an object, then in the **Material*&#x2A; section of the inspector click &#x2A;*+** and
choose **Cavity**.

***

## Controls [#controls]

| Control    | What it does                                                  |
| ---------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Ridge**  | How strongly raised edges and convex areas are picked out     |
| **Valley** | How strongly recesses, creases and concave areas are darkened |

Both can be used on their own. Valley alone reads as accumulated grime or ambient
shadow in the recesses; Ridge alone catches the light on the edges, like wear on a
raised lip.

***

## Tips [#tips]

* **Start with Valley.** Darkening recesses does more for readability than
  brightening ridges, and it's closer to how real surfaces age.
* **Keep it subtle.** High values on both look like an outline filter rather than
  surface shading.
* **It reads shape, not lighting.** The effect stays consistent as you move lights
  around, which makes it a dependable way to keep detail visible in a dim scene.
* Pairs well with the [Dust Layer](https://docs.spline.design/materials-shading/surface-detail/dust-layer.md) — dust on the
  upward faces, grime in the recesses.


# Displace Layer (/materials-shading/surface-detail/displace-layer)



The Displace Layer distorts the object's geometry (usually known as displacement).

This layer uses Noise Displacement (similar to how the Noise layer works, but it only distorts the geometry).

You can choose between different types of noise on the "Type" option.

<Video src="https://cdn.spline.design/_assets/docs/dbbd4095-dfe9-45b1-a961-fa22d52b2399.mp4" />


# Dust Layer (/materials-shading/surface-detail/dust-layer)

> Settle dust, snow or grime onto the upward-facing surfaces of an object.



The **Dust Layer** adds a colour to the surfaces of an object that face upwards,
the way dust, snow or ash settles in the real world. Steep and downward-facing
surfaces stay clear.

It's the fastest way to make an object look weathered or a scene look cold, without
painting a texture or adding geometry.

***

## Adding the layer [#adding-the-layer]

Select an object, then in the **Material*&#x2A; section of the inspector click &#x2A;*+** and
choose **Dust**.

***

## Controls [#controls]

| Control         | What it does                                                                                                                       |
| --------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Color**       | The colour of the settled dust — white for snow, grey for dust, dark for grime                                                     |
| **Coverage**    | How far down the sides the effect reaches. Low values leave only the flattest faces covered; high values creep down steeper slopes |
| **Softness**    | How sharply the covered area gives way to the bare surface. Low is a crisp snow line, high is a gradual fade                       |
| **Noise**       | Breaks up the edge so it isn't a perfectly even band                                                                               |
| **Noise Scale** | The size of that variation — small for fine grain, large for patchy drifts                                                         |

***

## Tips [#tips]

* **Snow:** white, high coverage, low softness, a little noise for a natural edge.
* **Dust on a shelf or machine:** light grey, low coverage, high softness.
* **Grime in crevices:** try the [Cavity Layer](https://docs.spline.design/materials-shading/surface-detail/cavity-layer.md)
  instead — it targets recesses rather than upward faces, and the two stack well.
* The effect follows the object's **orientation in the scene**, so rotating the
  object moves the dust to whatever now faces up.


# Normal Layer (/materials-shading/surface-detail/normal-layer)



In 3D geometry, the normals define the orientation of each of the triangles that form a shape.

The colors come from mix of the channels Y (green), X (red), and Z (blue). You can play around with the different channels and blending modes to add direction-based color tone changes to your 3D models.

<Video src="https://cdn.spline.design/_assets/docs/3c2b367a-9634-4322-b53a-75651243da86.mp4" />


# Outline Layer (/materials-shading/surface-detail/outline-layer)



The Outline Layer can be used to simulate hand-drawn style in your models and can be combined with the [Toon Layer](https://docs.spline.design/materials-shading/shading-and-reflection/toon-layer.md) to create cartoon-style 3D scenes.

<Embed src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/Ol6h9dyijew" title="YouTube video player" />

### Outline parameters [#outline-parameters]

You can adjust multiple parameters, such as the color and width of the outline. Increasing the threshold will add more outlines to the surface of the objects, especially the more complex ones. You can also change the smoothness of the lines and set if you want the outline to be only inside or both inside or outside.

### Contour parameters [#contour-parameters]

Increase the threshold value to apply lines to the surface of the object, you can also change the direction and projection type.

<Image src="https://cdn.spline.design/_assets/docs/32cdb5e7-5976-4bcb-895b-9b5578272b94.png" alt="image" maxWidth="300" />
